Author assumes price cartels, but this can be completely explained by supply and demand. Hyperscalars are buying all the storage they can get their hands on; that's it. Manufacturers are loath to ramp up supply (high CapEx), because they'll be underwater if the AI demand evaporates. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50663864&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350663864)_  |

You explained what a price cartel is. In a healthy economy for consumers, companies are supposed to compete against each others and adapt to the demand. If they all make the same choice to keep the supply low, it is a cartel. Increasing the supply does require a high CapEX, but they're expected to ramp up production of a product that can compete with rival companies' offerings. This is how you beat other companies, if and when your goal is to beat other companies. If all companies just decide to keep the supply low and be shy on investments, they're not in competition. I'm not denying that "not taking risks and keeping the supply low" is better for them financially, it's a very reasonable choice to make more money. I'm saying that's the moment when it's reasonable to accuse them of anti-competitive practices. Companies must take risks and attempt to beat the competition.Now I don't know if they're really keeping the production low for HDDs. But we have similar questions for GPUs, RAM and NAND memory. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665618&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665618)_  |

You've subtly misunderstood how markets work; they cannot make supply just appear without respect for the laws of physics based on a gut feel check by some man on the street that there is new persistent demand.Bringing new production capacity takes time. It's expensive. The people doing it have to make forecasts about what the state of the market is when the new supply will hit and (usually, anyway) require solid evidence that there is a long-term need before they commit to expensive options. If everyone is looking at the market and there is a wall-to-wall consensus that this might be a temporary demand spike then they're just being rational independent actors if they decide not to respond by bankrupting themselves expanding into a glut. In fact it must be quite common for markets to test if demand is real by requiring high prices to persist for a while before enough evidence accumulates and more investment in capacity happens. AFAIR if you look at a chart of long term prices for RAM or hard drives the cycles are very visible as prices rise and then new capacity and tech improvements come online. The overall trend shows healthy market forces. _EDIT_ Found the chart I was looking for. If this is a price cartel then IMO we need a few more of them because they seem to cause a really neat long term price dropping trend to form. <https://dam.stanford.edu/memory-prices.html> _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665849&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665849)_  |

This is where a market being an oligopoly is problematic. With only three players it’s a pretty safe bet that no one will take that bet. This is often implicit collusion. In a market with more players though, the chance that at least one company will make that gamble is much higher. The upside and amount of market you can gain is significant when you are a smaller player. Then other companies have to take into account the likelihood that other companies will be increasing supply, meaning they can’t safely rely on margins continuing to increase without investment. This all means a competitive industry will tend to be much more responsive to market conditions. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50667789&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350667789)_  |

I think Part of the problem is that, the storage market has some historical cartel-ish behavior, but they didn't get caught the same way DRAM/NAND makers have been caught.The most glaring case, was when just about every hard drive manufacturer except Samsung cutting their warranties to 1 year within a month of each other. (Thank goodness, Samsung had gotten good enough at making their stuff and was eager to get rid of the 'Samdung' reputation of techs.) It was a subtle but important change on some margin line, because yes some of those brands that changed policy did start to see higher failure rates when I was working at a shop slinging hardware. Thankfully we switched to primarily selling Samsung drives, especially since the way 'OEM' drive warranties worked, we would sometimes have to eat the cost if a drive was received by us, sat on a shelf for a month or two, and died in the last couple months of purchaser's first year. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50670348&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350670348)_  |

To be fair, octet is less ambiguous because it means 8 bits (oct-/octo- is a Greek prefix for eight), whereas some historical machines have used "bytes" that are not 8 bits. That's why the IETF uses "octet" in its documents, in English. But machines with 8-bit bytes have won decades ago, and "byte" is _the_ established standard word in the English language; you will never see an SSD or HDD marketed as "1 To" in English because it's always written as "1 TB". (And the only reason I even recognize "Go/To" at all is because of dual English+French labeling in Canada.) _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50669559&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350669559)_  |

The graph is great, and it shows the exponential drop of memory/storage over 70 years. The log scale is needed over such a timespan.But zoom in to the tail to see our pain, caused by the hyperscalers and AI companies and their investors buying up all the supply for years. Log scale hides it well, but the numbers are there: 
```
    DRAM: 0.898  $/GB in Aug 2023 -> 4.005   $/GB in Oct 2026 (4.46x higher)
    SSD:  0.0356 $/GB in Sep 2023 -> 0.08885 $/GB in Oct 2026 (2.49x higher)
    HDD:  0.0140 $/GB in Jul 2023 -> 0.02598 $/GB in Oct 2026 (1.85x higher)

```
(all prices in inflation-adjusted 2024 US dollars)Where the long-term trend is for prices to keep dropping, the past three years prices are _increasing_ -- doubling, quadrupling. We know the cause. Where does it stop? _Does_ it stop? Does 2023 become the end of the road, not for technological reasons, but for investment hype reasons? It's like saying capitalism is great if you just ignore the Great Depression. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50666556&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350666556)_  |

Look at P/E ratios for companies in these sectors:
```
    Sector   Company                     Trailing P/E
    -------  --------------------------  ------------
    DRAM     Samsung                            5.62x
    DRAM     SK Hynix                           4.19x
    SSD      Sandisk                            4.74x
    SSD      Kioxia                             5.19x
    HDD      Seagate                           24.77x
    HDD      Western Digital                   10.57x
    Coal     Coal India                         7.17x
    Coal     Alliance Resource Partners         9.60x

```
I included coal for comparison, because it's a highly cyclical, high capex, commodity industry, just like the DRAM, SSDs, and HDDs.You can see that the market does not expect flash & RAM prices to stay at this elevated level long-term. HDD prices are expected to remain somewhat elevated though. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50667055&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350667055)_  |

> It's like saying capitalism is great if you just ignore the Great Depression.The GD is a result of the government printing fiat money (starting in 1914) while retaining a fixed exchange rate for gold. By the time 1930 rolled around, due to inflation, gold was at a bargain price. This caused a run on the banks, resulting in their collapse. Eventually, FDR stopped that by making it illegal to buy gold. But by then, the damage was done. The next government failure was restricting the supply of money. People had no money. This problem was eventually resolved by massive armaments purchases from foreign countries in the late '30s. I.e. the GD was a failure due government mismanagement of the banks, not capitalism. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50669574&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350669574)_  |

> The overall trend shows healthy market forces.It does not show that at all. You can go read micron's 10-Q's and look at bits shipped and margins. IT looks like they spent the last few years minimizing supply till price was at an advantage. The ram makers (who also supply SSD capacity) are also back in court again for price fixing in public. That lawsuit is interesting because they cut supply on higher margin products in favor of lower margin higher volume ones to such a level that there will likely be shareholder involvement. This is before discussions of circular financing and all the special purpose vehicles that exist out there to prop up the market. There are bonds being sold at junk rates (north of 8 percent), where the guarantee on them is the underlying systems/chips that are being purchased. HBM is a massive part of the bill for a GPU installed at this point (and part of that bond coverage). The AI hopium (this is monetary, not utility) will return in the new year, and then crater late 28/29. EDIT: If your company isnt trying to hedge its cloud bill for next year, you should start asking hard questions of your CTO/CFO about how ram prices are going to impact your costs. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50666129&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350666129)_  |

> companies are supposed to compete against each others […] they’re expected to ramp up production […] Companies must take risks and attempt to beat the competition.There is no such rule. Why do you think this? You’re confusing description with prescription. When economists “expect” companies to compete, they’re talking macro-economics, not legal constraints. As many others have said, a cartel is when companies conspire to fix prices. If it happens on accident, which it does all the time, it’s just a market. More importantly, and for very good reasons, there is no requirement to beat competition and no requirement to ramp up production when demand surges. Having such a requirement would bankrupt many businesses and provide an anti-competitive advantage to larger businesses. Any company can freely choose whether they want to invest and ramp up production, and they can freely choose whether they want to compete with other companies. If high demand is temporary, then ramping up production is the wrong choice. If companies were required to compete, then a big business that can afford a temporary loss could out-price smaller businesses and force them out of business. This already happens, but making it some kind of legal requirement to avoid being accused of being a cartel and avoid anti-competitive legal problems, that would cement the monopoly advantage for the larger businesses into law. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50668507&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350668507)_  |

> When economists “expect” companies to compete, they’re talking macro-economics, not legal constraintsNo? We are talking about microeconomics, the behavior between individual agents. Macro is about aggregates across agents. Also, you are wrong while correcting the comment. Yes, he's confusing description with prescription, but the description itself describes a competitive market and something isn't happening here that may need a policy fix. In a competitive market when there's so much profit as these companies are reporting you would expect new competitors to enter the market until the marginal profit is equal or near zero. If that's not happening (and I'm not hearing anyone going into the magnetic plates business) you have to ask "why it isn't happening" and if the why is anticompetitive practices, financial constrains or legal barriers you fix for those. In a efficient economy, nobody is making bank. If the problems are the patents we have so many fixes, like forcing patent holders to license their technology to competitors on reasonable terms, breaking up or regulating patent pools that let incumbents cross license each other while freezing out newcomers, treating a dominant firm refusal to license as itself an abusive restraint on competition, etc. If the problem is financial then the solution is easy: access to credit or straight up buying companies to produce. If the problem is actually anticompetitive, breaking the companies in their funcional verticals/horizontals _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50668991&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350668991)_  |

> We are talking about microeconomics, the behavior between individual agents. Macro is about aggregates across agents.Yes, that was my point. Expecting companies to compete is something that happens in aggregate. That does not mean individual companies are required to compete. Parent claimed to be talking micro, but said something that only applies to macro. What am I wrong about exactly? The ‘description’ I was talking about was the parent’s stated implication that somehow companies are required to compete, otherwise they are cartels. There is no such line or requirement, and parent has confused what makes a cartel with what economists mean when they say companies are expected to compete. If you’re assuming I meant something else by “description”, you might have misunderstood. Having some suspicion that we might need a policy fix could mean we need a policy fix, or it could mean you’re making assumptions that turn out not to be true. The consumer side of a surge in demand sucks; prices go up. That doesn’t mean anything is wrong or that something requires fixing, it’s just a fact of markets. Someone needs to step in if there’s price fixing, but parent’s entire premise was that companies should be considered cartels and fixing prices even if they’re not communicating, and even if they’re accidentally matching prices, and if they’re not appearing to compete more strongly and trying to undercut the competition. I’m pointing out that this is a bad idea that if used, would directly lead to monopolies, not prevent them. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50669256&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350669256)_  |

Again, stop using macro/micro distinctions here. In macroeconomics we do not have a subset for hdd, most of the time it goes into "other industries". So, please, for the love of god, I'm going to hit you with my book of Das Kapital if you continue. There are 3 big groups, service, primary and secondary and hdd fabrication goes into the secondary in other industry subsector (unless your country has a big enough electronic industry to deserve its own subsector).Now that that's settled again, you are right that a surge in price doesn't require a fix, but a surge in price without anyone entering to compete for the economic profits to be had is something that deserves questions and shutting that discussion with just "it's just high demand that will settle" doesn't resolve it because it could also indicate structural weaknesses of the industry (like floods in a country suddenly cause prices everywhere to rise is a cause of concern about lack of resilience of the industry). So I ask again, because it seems that you ignored my list of potential responses from policy perspective: why isn't there a signal that anyone else is entering the market of making magnetic platter storage devices? BTW, nobody's proposing companies must compete or get treated as a cartel. You're arguing against a policy nobody proposed, we are asking why the market doesn't behave as expected of a supposedly highly comoditized market _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50670022&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350670022)_  |

You seem to be misunderstanding what both I and @iagiagiag said, and your point on macro - again - is agreeing with me, albeit violently. @iagiagiag did, in fact, claim that companies that don’t compete are considered cartel (and it wasn’t a proposal, they claimed it’s already the case). Before replying, go ahead and review, it’s right up there ^^.> why isn’t there a signal that anyone else is entering the market That question seems pretty naïve. Since you know about econ, I’m sure you already know there are some reasonably obvious answers to that question. How long does it take to create a new fab and enter the HDD market once a demand surge has started? The surge we have is ~1 year old. Why would it be smart to enter a market if the surge is temporary, and the manufacturers have been consolidating? (HDD manufacturers consolidated a decade before the current demand/price surge.) What are the costs and barriers to entry in the HDD market? Furthermore, we do have some competition signal. Toshiba is reported to be ramping up production. WD spun off its flash business and is focusing on HDD. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50671669&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350671669)_  |

No. From wikipedia: "A cartel is a group of independent market participants who _collaborate_ with each other [...] by creating artificial shortages"The total market demand of harddrives (and RAM) is higher than supply. Everybody can completely independently increase prices because they will still sell all their stock no matter that their prices are higher than the competition. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665816&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665816)_  |

I’m not fully buying your argument here; a ‘combination’ of companies ‘designed’ to limit competition pretty much does imply explicit coordination at some level. What you’re talking about is independent companies that were not designed to limit competition responding to each other without any coordination, which is legal and extremely common in free markets and does not amount to price fixing. It might be possible for some to follow the flock as long as is suits them and it can appear like coordination from afar, but that doesn’t prevent a company from breaking from the flock to gain an edge or because they can’t afford to follow forever or for other reasons. Without coordination, and given that following the competition incidentally is legal under anti-trust laws, I don’t believe that a company price-matching their competition is illegal, without further evidence of coordination. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50669727&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350669727)_  |

> _You explained what a price cartel is._ The word "cartel" generally means collaboration. In this case there could be no collaboration going on, and each dive maker is individually choosing not taking on the risk of more CapEx (which can be completely rational decision): > _A cartel is a group of independent market participants who collaborate with each other and avoid competing with each other in order to improve their profits and dominate the market._ * <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartel> > _This is how you beat other companies, if and when your goal is to beat other companies. If all companies just decide to keep the supply low and be shy on investments, they're not in competition._ They can be in competition, but each individually chooses not to try to be number one. Each is hoping _the others_ will beat each other up. See perhaps: > _A Stand Alone Complex is a social phenomenon where independent, unconnected individuals act in similar ways to create a seemingly coordinated effort or conspiracy, despite having no central leader, shared plan, or true original source._ Also: * <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_standoff> _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50668159&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350668159)_  |

No, they did not, they explained what supply and demand is. Cartels are where several companies agree to restrict supply, fix prices, agree to a maximum (rather than minimum) quality, or any other agreement between companies made purely to circumvent the market for financial gain.Some are considered legal, like OPEC, most are not, like the infamous Phoebus cartel that kept lightbulbs bad so they could sell more bulbs per year, and some come about unintentionally, like the US potato cartel naturally coming about due to everyone using the same service for algorithmically pricing the same product for different companies. A cartel would be if all the HDD manufacturers got together and went "looking at the current market, let's all agree to 3x our prices, but keep the relative differences so as not to make it obvious". If that agreement exists, cartel. If not, no cartel, just corporations doing what corporations do. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50667770&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350667770)_  |

> I'm not denying that "not taking risks and keeping the supply low" is better for them financiallyThen you're admitting it's not a price cartel. A cartel, by definition, requires companies to ignore their _individual_ incentives to increase supply and undercut others in order to fix prices _collectively_. This case is the opposite -- it's being driven entirely by the fact that none of them wants to take the individual risk of being out over a ledge if the bubble pops. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50670615&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350670615)_  |

I find AI generated code to be more efficient overall, mostly because it doesn’t have the readability requirements as human code. Also, traditionally “easy” human programming languages perform very poorly with LLMs.I write a lot of PHP (I know). PHP is not only slow, it’s awful for LLMs because it’s so dynamic. It’s difficult to statically analyze. So, there’s basically no reason to program in PHP outside of “we already have code in PHP”. It performs worse, and it’s harder to write and verify. But, AI generated PHP code gives a lot more weight to types. So, it ends up being easier to read and more performant than human written code typically. This is still a step up from JS, which is even _more_ dynamic and wishy-washy than PHP. But, the best languages for LLMs have strict, high-quality, and expansive tool chains. Like dotnet, Java, and rust. These languages are also much higher performance than “ergonomic” languages like PHP, ruby, python, JS. So, we will see more of them over the coming years I think. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50669755&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350669755)_  |

The fundamental problem is that no one believes that AI demand is durable. This isn’t AI skepticism, it’s a belief that we’re in a bubble and that there will be a consolidation at some point where a lot of hypers-scalars go out of business and are bought at fire-sale prices.This lack of being able to forecast reliable demand far enough out to get an ROI on increased capacity fully explains why producers are just happy to ride the wave of higher prices rather than taking a huge risk to capture a larger chunk of the market. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50669164&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350669164)_  |

Yeah it is a bit different when you talk, for example, about oil and OPEC (an actual cartel). Because there is no new investment needed to pump production up so there is a big risk of break-away from the group. So they need to formalize the arrangement and monitor the participants of the cartel (it has been widely reported of OPEC members covertly selling more than they were supposed to).Compared to electronics, setting up a new fab is incredibly expensive, if demand dries up you lose a lot of money. And it is not like you can hide a fab and its output capacity. It is not a commodity (in the sense you can mix it with other global supply to hide where it comes from), everything that comes out of it has your company label on. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665442&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665442)_  |

> all of the manufacturers lose profit margins if any of them ramp up supply significantly.You've just described the incentive to create a cartel... The way a theoretical efficient market works is that this collaboration is forbidden and each company marginally ramp up supply whenever that action creates more profit for them (at the expense of the other companies profits, and the total profit the industry collects). Iterated this results in them _all_ significantly ramping up supply and having exactly zero profit (which rather explains why real markets look nothing like theoretical efficient ones). _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50667943&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350667943)_  |

> Why do chipmakers engage in this behavior but not supermarkets, airliners and restaurants?Part of norways high cost of living, especially when compared to neighbours like finland, sweden, and denmark, is actually because of grocery stores engaging in such behaviour. There's only 3 or 4 national level suppliers for the supermarkets (despite there supposedly being a lot of supermarket "types"), so all their prices are basically the same. And much higher than in other european countries. Tolls ensure the oligopoly remains in place without competition from other european suppliers. So, it very much can happen. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50671785&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350671785)_  |

Making supermarkets, airliners, and restaurants has a far lower barrier to entry, in both money and expertise, than a facility that manufactures chips (or HDD or other complicated electronics). Multiple orders of magnitude lower.Note that making (big) airplanes has a similarly high barrier to entry, and hence why only a few organizations exist that make them, just like chips and HDD. But airliners are, comparatively, much lower barrier to entry. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50667090&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350667090)_  |

If you want to go down memory lane, read about price collusion in LCD monitors from the early 2000s which involved most LCD manufacturers and suppliers at the time.This is a good start: [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TFT-LCD_(Flat_Panel)_Antitrust...](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TFT-LCD_\(Flat_Panel\)_Antitrust_Litigation) These things really do happen. They ended up getting trialed in multiple countries and pleaded guilty, it wasn't a conspiracy. With SSDs, RAM and GPUs, there's a lot of incentives for AI cloud providers to keep these out of consumer hands to avoid local LLMs becoming a real thing since that directly competes with their revenue models. It's not impossible to think manufacturers of these hardware components are getting paid off to shift / limit supply and keep prices unreasonably high for the masses. GPU manufacturers have an extra benefit of not selling you GPUs too because renting their GPUs in the cloud is more profitable for them. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50666644&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350666644)_  |

Almost everyone gets a negotiated discount off of the list price. Higher volume buyers get bigger discounts. Allocations are decided on volumes and not on a dollars per GPU basis. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665403&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665403)_  |

> Allocations are decided on volumes and not on a dollars per GPU basis.Yeah and Nvidia is always going to favor the one single player that just says "here, 10 billion dollars in straight cash, you give me all the GPUs you can" versus a bunch of small players (especially if NVIDIA is invested into the large players - they, after all, want their investment to go up). For society as a whole however, the GPUs sitting around is inefficient waste as there are so many companies and invididuals that could _actually use_ these GPUs (or RAM chips or whatnot) to make money. That is why I want to see the hoarders punished. They are using their immediately available cash to drown out everyone else. And Sam Altman, who played RAM manufacturers behind closed doors and single-handedly wrecked the RAM market, should be locked up and the keys thrown away. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665554&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665554)_  |

"intentional throttling of production" (or product lifespan) sounds like an issue of unpriced idle capital. Since idle capital is inevitable, you have to figure out how to price it back into the one time sales your entire business runs on. The customers then complain because of the backhanded way the idle capital is priced in, but that same customer would probably scoff at paying for idle capital to begin with. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665639&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665639)_  |

Lithography machines are generally not taken out of production, they just go down the value chain. Some 200mm wafer size tools are now considered so obsolete that they have been removed from use, but nearly all 300mm wafer tools, dating back to 1997, are still in active use. As a tool becomes unable to do leading edge stuff, it starts getting used to do something a bit more pedestrian.> like, flash storage doesn't necessarily need to get bigger or faster at this point (imo), 1 TB SSDs will serve a big % of the markets. I think you are very wrong on this, the amount of media we consume and the space it needs is still growing rapidly, but even if you are not, a 1TB SSD still has at least 4 NAND FLASH dies. If better litho lets you build one with fewer dies, you will effectively get more SSDs out of the same wafer. NAND is mostly scaling by increasing layers, though. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665147&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665147)_  |

Memory, both chips and HDs, eventually collapses in price; that’s been the cycle for decades. The boom-and-bust cycle is what has driven the number of manufacturers down. My suspicion is that, in a few years, the price per gig will collapse again. AI is memory-hungry, and PC manufacturers will be pushing self-hosted AI models, which will require lots of memory. I bet we’ll see an increase in density per unit, which will bring down the price per gig. I give it five years at most. At that time, we will be seeing PC's capable of hosting a full AI model for the equivalen of less that $1000 today. It's how tech has worked for decades. There's no reason to think things have changed. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50662602&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350662602)_  |

>There's no reason to think things have changed.There are a few: - A massive expansion in userbase for what previously would have been considered high-performance computing equipment. Now that no particular skills whatsoever are required to harness these to some productive end, demand is presumably seeing a step-change up. - Concentration/consolidation in manufacturing, largely unchecked by the partial lack of national, and complete lack of international guardrails against collusion (price fixing, supply limiting, etc) - The increase in capex required to manufacture commercially viable semiconductors in volume - The practical cessation of semiconductor process technology advancement (compared to the '80s, '90s, and '00s, say) Taken together, when you say you "give it five years at most", I suspect you are right when it comes to the _rate of increase of price_ (of bits of RAM, flash, TFLOPs of compute, etc) but I do not think we will see continuing exponential decreases in cost - or if we do, that exponent will itself have been greatly diminished. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50663311&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350663311)_  |

IMO commodity DRAM prices will collapse quickly once inference moves off it.Initially into weights on flash, using some advanced flash packaging to increase throughput. HBF for servers, something like zNAND-O, MSA-CBA, Xtacking, etc for client, client side has not yet cohered around a standard. Eventually advanced upcoming stacked memory, like IGZO 2T0C DRAM stacked on logic, FeFETs stacked on logic, etc. DRAM is not the platonic ideal where to store model weights. They are inherently RMM ("Read Mostly Memory"), with extreme throughput demands, and very predictable usage pattern. And specifically, memory use is extremely local to the compute units using them, at the extreme a fully scaled out inference apparatus would be most efficient with hundred thousand local pools instead of one global one. We are currently serving our models from commodity DRAM because the entire industry is extremely young, and that's what we had available. Once the dust settles, better architectures will take it's place. The KV-cache will continue to use commodity DRAM until something does the RW load better than it. FLASH prices are only going to keep going up in the short term, because HBF will do to FLASH what HBM did to memory (less bits out of the same fab capacity, much more demand for those bits). That too shall pass eventually. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665271&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665271)_  |

> FLASH prices are only going to keep going up in the short term, because HBF will do to FLASH what HBM did to memoryHBM consumed RAM manufacturing capacity and the same will happen to flash with HBF. It’s just that the dies will not be encapsulated and sold on M.2 sticks but piled up into HBF and sold for integration into modules. It’s either demand that drops for some reason magical or not, or new capacity comes online. In the case of HDDs, which is what the article is about, I don’t think companies are willing to invest in the R&D needed to outcompete flash because, at some point, the mechanics needed to pack, say, 50 TB into a 3.5” form factor leave the mechanical engineering domain and arrive at physics, where advances become increasingly hard. Doubling density again to 100TB per drive might just not be feasible. At the same time, areal density of non-volatile solid-state memory might will continue to improve, and, with it, manufacturing prices will go down faster than for HDDs. HDDs are a dead end. I wouldn’t invest much R&D in them. All three surviving manufacturers know that and are acting accordingly. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50666574&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350666574)_  |

DRAM doesn't need the same feature size as logic (capacitors need to be a certain size), and Apple qualified their DDR5 - and then CXMT declined to sell to them because their domestic market is more than big enough.CXMT is already making HBM. The article about that, which included the 1930 to 1932 timeline for transforming a prototype into equipment suitable for mass production, which seems realistic, was adjacent inside some Chinese news site to another article about China that was the subject of a HN thread a week or two ago.However, trying to find it again now by googling, because I do not remember the HN thread, did not succeed quickly because the search returned tons of pages that seem to be AI slop that repeats more or less the same things, but originating in older press releases, not in that one. In any case, the subject of the article was a prototype demo made in October at the China International Industry Fair in Shanghai, by the company Langdao Silex, and the timeline was attributed to a declaration of that company. Another article about the same event is: [https://www.semiconinsider.com/2026/10/15/langdao-silex-unve...](https://www.semiconinsider.com/2026/10/15/langdao-silex-unveils-prototype-euv-light-source-lithography/) They seem to already have an acceptable EUV light source, but from that to an EUV lithography machine there is a long path, with the production of the reflective optical components being one of the most difficult parts to achieve. China appears to be now at about the same level of EUV technology that was ever achieved in USA, i.e. at the level where Cymer was before being acquired by ASML. ASML needed almost a decade to develop the light source from Cymer into a usable EUV lithography machine, with the cooperation of many other European companies, e.g. Zeiss, but China will need only about half of that, because the path is now known and enough money and people will be supplied to this to accelerate the development. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50666045&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350666045)_  |

MU has ~80% gross margins for a commodity. Yes, it takes time and investment to build out capacity, but those types of margins simply will not last.The demand side is also becoming capital constrained with liquidity getting harder to come by. Peak prices are set at the margin. A small shift in supply and/or demand can have an outsized move on prices. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50664106&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350664106)_  |

> The practical cessation of semiconductor process technology advancement (compared to the '80s, '90s, and '00s, say)It's not a cessation, it's a slight deceleration. In 90s the number of transistor doubled every two years, nowadays it takes 3-4 years. It's still an exponent just not as steep as it was. Processes advance two - today's chips are 2nm and in the next year 1nm are expected. I would actually want the semiconductor industry to stabilise and not push a new process every year or two but it doesn't happening so far. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50664452&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350664452)_  |

At launch.As the processes mature, they still get cheaper over time, they are just no longer immediately cheaper. Despite initially being more expensive than older processes, N6 is now the cheapest $/transistor process in the industry, and even though N3 started out much more expensive, it is steadily trending down to beat it. It just means that the new reality is that new nodes are first used for premium products that are used to amortize the lines and work up the yields, but over time the transistor costs do keep falling for other product segments. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665353&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665353)_  |

Prices are set at the margin. If capacity gets overbuilt as it always does in such bubbles, we’ll see a collapse in price as soon as customers who placed the orders start canceling their projects and offloading their now homeless hardware. Technological pace of improvement (or lack of it) is in the short term completely overpowered by basic market dynamics and fundamental economics: spot price is set by the last transaction.(Note that ram prices are already hinting at least stabilization. The models we’re in awe with which solve millennium problems are trained on hardware ordered before the bubble, some of it may have been installed this year, not sure. Marginal component price is now determined by datacenters which aren’t even in the hole-in-the-ground phase and may be canceled at any time.) _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50663731&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350663731)_  |

I’ve been into computers since I was a kid and I expect I’ll enjoy it till I die. A several year stretch of high prices might slow my upgrade cadence for a while, but the doomerism about the end of the hobby is getting overblown.The high spec computer I want to buy is expensive right now, but I’ve been scrolling Black Friday deals and there have been a lot of good deals on respectable gaming pre-builts coming through. The people who will settle for nothing less than 128GB of RAM next to their RTX 5090 are going to be unhappy, but a good laptop or decent gaming rig is still very attainable. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50663132&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350663132)_  |

The way I see it, Facebook and Google (no, I refuse to call them by their newly made up names) especially are in a good spot because they had tens of billions of dollars in net profit and free cash flow every year before the AI build out so they can afford to service their debt at least somewhat. Also either everybody — including them can continue building or nobody can build anymore. Both cases are ok for Facebook and Google.It is important to remember that these two companies in particular didn't have an option to sit it out, unlike Apple. So they did what they had to however insane it feels to basically dump money into this furnace. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50663081&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350663081)_  |

> They're not going to get _less_ hungry for compute over time when there's ever more money to be made.Compute is now a market with its own supply and demand. Compute capacity takes a long time to be built out, so it’s possible that compute construction overshoots demand for a while. That’s a big open question from the finance angle right now: There is so much money committed to compute buildouts that the companies will have to find ways to drive demand for it. It’s also possible this goes the other way and demand overshoots even the aggressive buildout plans for a while. Long term, as long as there is demand for consumer hardware there will eventually be manufacturing capacity for it. We may not get the bleeding edge node, fastest CPUs, GPUs, and SSDs into consumer gaming rigs and laptops right away, but where there’s demand, there will eventually be supply. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50663108&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350663108)_  |

I was alive :), but that era was very different.At the start of the 80s PCs were expensive. Demand was (by today's standards) really really low. Prices around $5000 were not uncommon. As factories got built, as as the tech progressed (very quickly) the prices came down (think $2000) but a 2 year old machine was pretty much obsolete. Disk space, ram, cpu, cdrom drives, everything was doubling every year or so. Obviously there was a lot of profit to be had so production scaled up, and prices dropped. Machines also lasted a lot longer. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50663080&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350663080)_  |

What do you mean by "there was not as much productive use for hardware"? In personal computing there has been productive use for hardware since 1979 (VisiCalc) and of course even before that in the entreprise context. For context the spreadsheet made finance, accounting, business management work 80x faster with less errors. It was a huge productivity boost, and it pushed hardware sales up.The chip and memory have always been the limiting factor, and in the 80s the WIMP GUIs needed more powerful hardware to open up new productive usage (including totality transforming publishing, paper publishing at the time) _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665705&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665705)_  |

Silicon chip production is under supply-chain suppression from data-center speculative projects.What to expect if bubble remains inflated: 1. consumer DRAM/VRAM prices to be kept high by scalpers till 2030 2. quality SSD flash drives to remain inflated till 2032 China based firms are ramping up production, and international markets expect to continue shifting over to more budget friendly brands within the next few quarters. For domestic markets, building on US soil now incurs liabilities other jurisdictions simply don't see. Have a nice day, =3 _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50662698&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350662698)_  |

Yep, at the start of the year I bought a 8-bay UNAS before the HD price increases had fully “set” in my brain. It still sits empty because I can’t afford to put drives in it.I have my hard drive purchase info back a little over a decade and for the past few years (before 2025) I was settling into ~$14/TB. Now it’s _well_ over double that. The only Seagate Exos 20TB (not refurb) on Amazon I can find is $999, ~$50/TB. It’s a hard time to be homelab-er. I’m eternally grateful that my main server is only like 4-5 years old. I have friends that were forced to upgrade and the RAM costs alone made me sick. In one case they bought half the RAM my server has and paid twice what I paid (aka 4x increase). That said, I’ve never been happier with self-hosting but that makes this harder since I’d love to expand my home lab. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50667017&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350667017)_  |

The solution to AI driven cyberattacks on your home infrastructure is to use AI to harden your home infrastructure against said attacks.All of the things that are hard to setup for a single lone homelabber are trivial for an AI agent to setup. Going from zero to passably enterprise grade infrastruture has never been faster or easier. Hand it a few SSH certificates for admin accounts on a few Raspberry Pis, an explanation of your network map, and tell it what you want. In your case, there is no need for complex client certificate or WireGuard nonsense. Just setup actual SSO accounts for your friends and family. Now their OS/client is responsible for keeping a hold of their password, they can password reset themselves, enroll in 2FA, and you can be very granular about permissions. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50670107&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350670107)_  |

```
    > FY 2025: Net loss of -$25.61M USD

    > FY 2023: Net loss of -$59.71M USD
    > FY 2022: Net loss of -$51.40M USD
    > FY 2021: Net loss of -$21.70M USD

```
What terrible performance!
```
    > Do you think it's a growth business?

```
I found this from their homepage:
```
    > According to the Backblaze About Page, the company oversees more than 6.12 exabytes (6,120+ petabytes) of data for over 500,000 customers across 175+ countries.

```
They are adding about one petabyte of storage per year. That looks like a good growth trajectory to me, but they are consistently losing money (year-over-year). _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50666510&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350666510)_  |

well, personally, my job has become so easy because of AI and my level of experience in my domains that I'm learning low-level programming as a way to keep myself sharp and expand my horizons with all the extra time I have. I've been going to a bunch of conferences this year and have met lots of people with similar mindsets.Digging into things like game engines and networking and programming languages I'm finding my way into many bustling online communities & open source projects that are seeing more and more people join every day. These communities are filled with deeply interested and intelligent people who are continuing to engage with computing and not just orchestrating agents. Obviously AI usage is pushing people upwards, and the entire history of computing is largely on big movement up the stack, so obviously that general trend isn't going anywhere, but it is also easier than ever to learn and work close to the metal and many people are taking advantage of that. Now that we find ourselves in a once again resource constrained world (hardware and VPS are all suddenly much more expensive), learning to program with a mind for mechanical sympathy is becoming more important if you want to eek performance and cost savings out of your work. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50671657&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350671657)_  |

I believe most people are using SAS expanders. SATA drives work in SAS ports (the reverse is not true) although not every combination is well tested, and you can get double the ports because normal SAS is dual-channel.Yout machine probably doesn't have a SAS card (HBA) in it so you'll need to get one. You also need special breakout cables that bring one SAS port to 4 separate SATA plugs. There are also different connectors used with SAS so you have to be aware of that. You'll probably have plenty of ports on your HBA alone (often 32 per card) but if not, you can make a tree of expanders to connect a very large number of drives. The PCIe-shaped ones usually take power from PCIe but don't connect data, so they don't need to actually go inside a computer. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50668228&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350668228)_  |

Re-encoding will save you here. At a generous 15mbps bitrate a 100TB drive could store 600 continuous days of video, or 150 days of your quad camera ride footage. As low as 6mbps could be acceptable for raw footage you just want to keep around.I throw all my drone footage that isn’t going into a produced video through handbrake before adding it to expensive cloud storage. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50664422&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350664422)_  |

There is a continuum of data importance. At one end might be family pictures and legal documents. At the other end might be a movie torrent which can be easily downloaded again. As cloud storage prices increase incrementally, the point at which people prefer cloud storage moves on the continuum. I think a lot of people are using cloud storage for data they don’t _really_ care about that much. There is a lot of room to move a lot of data local.I use cloud for the really important stuff, but for my movies and shows, I rely on RAID. If the server somehow gets destroyed I’ll be sad but it’s not the end of the world. That risk is worth it to me for saving thousands of dollars every month in cloud storage. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50664166&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350664166)_  |

These things happen in cycles. Blu-ray is my current solution to the problem. It occurs to me that I really don't need most of my data to be either writeable, or online. I index them with MeoFinder, and so far, have been perfectly happy with the arrangement. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50666607&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350666607)_  |

I like to think of this as a natural extension of the DRAM pricing crisis.More and more engineers are probably exploring their ability to page memory out to disk given the cost of DRAM. We're effectively pushing the cost up the stack of storage latency. HBM => DRAM => NVMe => spinning rust Tape and optical media are the last stop on this train ride. We're almost there. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665053&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665053)_  |

Clayton Christensen’s book _Innovator’s Dilemma_ (which was based partly on his own PhD thesis) is based squarely on the falling prices (and how!) or storage and memory, and how different companies ignored the revolution in their own industry, one by one.I recall that, every time I see the eye watering RAM and SSD/HDD prices today. Supply, demand, and all those economic laws… Am wondering if the price/TB will ever fall down as it had been for several decades. Or what it would take for that trend to continue _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50663652&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350663652)_  |

General bloat computing? I used to say you can't fill up your disks with software. A whole Linux appliance was around 100 MB, and that was not a tiny one , but processing videos. Nowadays a banking app can be 400 MB, every golang binary 40 MB. Add CI/CD running countless containers.The amount of video is not flat either. Edit: The other question is: How much is demand-driven. Maybe it's also supply-side. The 3 remaining players keeping supply too low for better margins? _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50662501&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350662501)_  |

Still cheaper than S3 / GCS / ABS!$10 to $25/TB to store in one of those places, paid every month -- with the nerve to charge an additional $10/TB or so just to retrieve it at the lower end of that bracket. The "high" $27.50/TB in the article shows how much margin there is on these products. Sure, you might want to factor in replication and the server hardware, but you're still looking at paying it off in a few months with real hardware. S3 is still the same pricing as 10 years ago, and the cost to egress that out to the internet is effectively over $27/Mbps/month -- what someone would have paid for internet transit about 20 years ago. You can buy that transit for $0.02 to $0.08/Mbps/month these days, meaning it's nearly 1000x what you'd pay elsewhere. Let me put it another way: You can get a 100Gbps transit port for $2000 to $8000/month, capable of serving around 10,000,000 GB (assuming you fill that connection only to 33%), or you can get about 25,000 GB with the big three cloud providers. The hardware comes to about $50 to $70/TB, the rack space and power are relatively cheap. Not just that, with your own build you can get an actual file system, not just blob storage. Hell, you can even lease a dedicated server from Hetzner / OVH / whoever for about $2 to $3/TB/month if you didn't want to handle any of the hardware or networking portions of it. As far as I can tell, the main reason there isn't any real competition on the data storage front is that if your compute is in one of those clouds, it can cost you literally the same to send those bytes to the remote storage as it would be to store those bytes for months using the cloud provider's offering. I'm really looking forward to a resurgence in non-cloud providers again, it's long overdue. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50663281&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350663281)_  |

 | ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif)  | [](https://news.ycombinator.com/vote?id=50665510&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646#50665510)  |  [adrian_b](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=adrian_b) [10 hours ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50665510) | [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50661646#50662131) | [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50661646#50664142) | [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50661646#50662553) [[–]](javascript:void\(0\))   
Desktop motherboards have much less PCIe slots than in the past.The ATX MB format allows for 6 16-lane PCIe 5.0 slots, but you will not see that, except with server CPUs. Desktop CPUs support at most 1 full slot or 2 half-width slots at full speed. Besides the 1 or 2 PCIe 5.0 slots, many motherboards have only 1 or 2 slots that are too slow (e.g. by being 1-lane PCIe 4.0) for things like a fast network card usable to interconnect 2 desktop computers, e.g. for doing inference with an LLM using both. Only few motherboards have 1 or at most 2 4-lane PCIe 4.0 slots, that could be used for not too slow interconnections. So in desktop motherboards, I do not think that there exists currently any motherboard that offers more than 4 fast PCIe slots, where 2 slots are 8-lane PCIe 5.0 and 2 slots are 4-lane PCIe 4.0, but sharing a single 4-lane PCIe 4.0 link with the CPU. So the aggregated PCIe throughput in modern motherboards is pathetic in comparison with what was available in desktop motherboards from a decade ago. Now the desktop motherboards are designed only for supporting 1 or 2 GPUs and 2 to 4 SSDs, but with bandwidth sharing for the upper number of devices, with no provision for fast interconnections between desktops, beyond what you can get by linking 2 desktops with a Thunderbolt cable, if they have appropriate Thunderbolt ports. If you want to use e.g. 25 Gb/s Ethernet NICs, which are not much more expensive than 10 Gb/s NICs, you must give up on GPUs or SSDs, to take PCIe slots from them. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50665510&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350665510)_  |

Yeah that's part of the issue with conspiracy theories. It can be easy to point at a specific person or group but that usually just ends up shifting the blame away from real systemic issues. People get fired up, "things are bad because {person} is evil and making it bad, if we get rid of them everything will be good again." But really the issues will persist regardless of who is the face of it at the time. It's so much harder to reflect on how things got so bad and what can be done to prevent it happening again. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50662415&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350662415)_  |

Market socialism can exist in theory and existed in practice in Yugoslavia. It does not exist today in Europe. Every European economy allows private businesses and individuals to own the means of production and freely allocate capital. And since we’re bringing America into the picture, many (Denmark, Switzerland, Irelans) actually have fewer regulations on private capital and fewer restrictions on economic freedom than the US does. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50668066&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350668066)_  |

The upfront cost stinks but building a high-capacity data hoard was already a big luxury. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50662336&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350662336)_  |

Every time people talk about consumers setting prices, they mean setting prices _way below cost_. This doesn't make sense, "exchange value" _is the word_ Marx used to describe what we call a commodity in English. A commodity doesn't _have_ an exchange value, it is one. In general, people now must make a clear distinction between storage and archiving.HDD above 4TB are now often always shingled writes, and have a shorter service life < 3 years of fly time. Above that, folks should always have a +2 spare on ZFS, and run the bit-rot detection process weekly. For JBOD, the 8TB HDD is still the cheapest $/TiB option I found. If you don't have the rack space, than consider the higher capacity drives as a consumable operational expense. Even the cheapest NVMe SSD will last 7+ years if treated properly. If powered on every month for auto-repairs. OverlayFS root or a logging filesystem like F2FS will almost double the life of the drive to 12+ years. Note, even if the SSD costs 300% more upfront, the reliability of a good brand means your drive failure rates are much lower (if and only if you are not hammering a log on storage like the recent nvidia driver was doing.) 18TB LTO9 Tape is still the cheapest $/TiB backup option, but the drives/auto-loaders have a high initial fixed cost... The used units also tend to be near the end of their service life (often not worth the price.) For personal workflows, I spun up dual BD-R writers (omnidrive/libredrive firmware) for cheap bulk 25G offline storage discs like it was 2017 (with the odd 100GB bdxl disc for firmware images). It was a trade-off for cheap media, 10+ year data retention rating (M-disc last longer), and tolerable data transfer rates at 6x. It is slow i/o, requires manual loading, and is not an option for most performance applications. It is great because ODD suck in high-performance applications, so are not hit by hyper-scaling shenanigans. ymmv =3 _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50662508&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350662508)_  |

Don't scrub weekly. Bit rot at rest is rare and happens over long timescales; the odds of it striking the same record on three different drives in a week span has to be almost nil. On the other hand duty cycles for HDDs are surprisingly low. My "NAS" Seagates are 20TB with 300TB/year duty cycles; that's only 15 full scrubs a year assuming no other activity. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50662703&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350662703)_  |

On some high-capacity data-center drives CMR is no longer even supported, and the driver must buffer in several hundred MB chunks at a time to finish a write properly. > On some high-capacity data-center drives CMR is no longer even supportedWhich high-capacity drives are these? Seagate's IronWolf (NAS) drives are all CMR (up to the current top of the range 18TB). Seagate's IronWolf Pro (NAS) drives are all CMR (up to the current top of the range 30TB). Seagate's EXOS (datacenter) drives are all CMR (up to the current top of the range 30TB). WD's Red (NAS [1]) drives are all SMR but top out at 6TB, hardly high capacity. WD's Red Plus (NAS) drives are all CMR (up to the current top of the range 12TB, again hardly high). WD's Red Pro (NAS) drives are all CMR (up to the current top of the range 26TB). WD's Gold (datacenter) drives are all CMR (up to the current top of the range 24TB). Toshiba's DT (desktop) series drives are all SMR but top out at 6TB (again hardly high). Toshiba's N300 (NAS) series drives are all CMR (up to the current top of the range 22TB). Toshiba's MG (datacenter) series drives are all CMR (up to the current top of the range 24TB). [1] Yeah, right. _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50663866&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350663866)_  |

>[1] Yeah, right.Perhaps, but you can't just look at the brand label to know for sure... Every manufacture has different model number suffix used to identify the drive configuration. They usually do not advertise the specifics on the packaging. Drive-Managed SMR (dm-smr) abstracts the slow/random-latency interface as a classic LBA drive. Some older model number suffix to watch out for: [https://rossmanngroup.com/technical-reference/cmr-vs-smr-har...](https://rossmanngroup.com/technical-reference/cmr-vs-smr-hard-drives) Tips for spotting the obfuscated features in modern SATA/SAS drives: 1. abnormally large dram buffers on relatively small disk capacities 2. a HDD supporting SSD trim functions 3. sudden i/o performance loss when writing over 1% of the drive capacity (need to dd flood the CMR zone normally used to cache hot/busy areas of the filesystem like the journal, and force flushing to the SMR zone) 4. Cheap consumer drives hitting high-capacity claims with suspiciously low 3.5" drive platter counts (anything claiming above 3.0TB/platter is likely distracting from the superparamagnetic limit): <https://rml527.blogspot.com/> Modern drives may skip unpredictable dm-cmr mode of operation for host managed hm-smr. 30TB Ultrastar DC HC690 sales contact: [https://www.westerndigital.com/en-ca/products/internal-drive...](https://www.westerndigital.com/en-ca/products/internal-drives/data-center-drives/ultrastar-dc-hc690-hdd?sku=WSH723200AL4201) UltraSMR brief: [https://documents.westerndigital.com/content/dam/doc-library...](https://documents.westerndigital.com/content/dam/doc-library/en_us/assets/public/western-digital/collateral/tech-brief/tech-brief-ultrasmr-technology.pdf) White paper: [https://documents.westerndigital.com/content/dam/doc-library...](https://documents.westerndigital.com/content/dam/doc-library/en_us/assets/public/western-digital/collateral/white-paper/white-paper-implementing-smr.pdf) You must be very lucky with your choices... Have a wonderful day. =3 _[reply](https://news.ycombinator.com/reply?id=50664745&goto=item%3Fid%3D50661646%2350664745)_  |
